- **#27** *(iop)* RESOLVED, not by the reading it was written for. The question was whether a DK transaction is dispatched inside a bus-6 silence -- i.e. whether FCOS dispatches and the MSC starts the BCE yet nothing reaches the wire (service starvation live), or nothing is dispatched at all (fault upstream in dispatch). NEITHER: the transaction IS dispatched, the BCE DOES run, and it FINISHES in ~0.3 ms. The silence is the wire being legitimately idle after the work is done, while the completion takes ~1053 ms to arrive.
  - evidence: See entry 22. w3's BWTRACE shows #WAT at +0.29 ms into a 1052.64 ms hold, identical to a 0.59 ms hold. Note the earlier v91a analysis of this question was inconclusive because the SIO it found 'inside' each silence sat 0.02-0.03 ms before the silence ENDED -- it was the NEXT transaction's dispatch, not one inside the gap.
  - addrs `152-152` &middot; csects `FIOCMPLT,FIOMNTR` &middot; procs `BCE6,BCE7,BCE8,BCE18,MSC` &middot; phases `8` &middot; tape `v36` &middot; env `YAGPC_BUSLOG` &middot; files `src/mmumodel.c,src/iop.c` &middot; symptom `1053 ms bus hold,pool overflow` &middot; tsec `391-400` &middot; run `v91a,w3`
- **#30** *(method)* Bus 18's multi-second continuous activity during the stall (921,749 events, 292,074 RECV_POLL over 8.54 s, vs 1,833 events / 9 polls for a normal transfer) is a runaway BCE read loop.
  - evidence: NOT an anomaly. In the bus log RECV_POLL/RECV_WORD are emitted once per 16.5 us wheel revolution while a receive is pending, so the counts measure SERVICE TICKS, not words -- 1822 events over 30.13 ms is exactly one per revolution. Each bus-18 burst matches its own MMU READY->0..READY->1 interval exactly (26 blocks: 651 ms ready-gap vs 654 ms burst; 123 blocks: 3109 ms; 115 blocks: 2906 ms; ~25 ms/block throughout). The bus-18 activity is a large read taking the time a large read takes. Do not read the bus-log event counts as word counts.
  - procs `BCE18` &middot; tape `v36` &middot; env `YAGPC_BUSLOG` &middot; files `src/run.c,gpc-buslog.py` &middot; tsec `390-401` &middot; run `v91a`
- **#36** *(method)* RETRACTED (was: 'make while a run is in flight kills it'). It does NOT. v93a and v94a were still running normally after the rebuild -- clang unlinks and recreates the output, so a running process keeps its old inode. They looked dead because gpc.log is EMPTY UNTIL EXIT (the SIMULATED TIME line is written on stop) and because a ps|grep check returned 0 spuriously.
  - evidence: Both were found alive minutes later, at 12:40 and 04:56 elapsed, and on SIGINT wrote SIMULATED TIME 584.5 s and 301.6 s. What IS true and did cost time: (1) a nohup'd headless-gpcmem.sh can be orphaned so its 'sleep SECS; kill -INT' never fires, leaving an emulator running indefinitely and its port base held, which makes the next launch fail with 'a run is already on port base N'; (2) an empty gpc.log means STILL RUNNING, not crashed -- judge progress from deu.log; (3) a scanner using re.search(r't=([0-9.]+)') silently matches the 't=0' inside 'prot=0' and reports 0, so anchor it as \\st=.
  - files `pass-run/headless-gpcmem.sh` &middot; run `v93a,v94a`
- **#39** *(iop)* REFUTED as a mechanism -- the MSC does NOT park in an 8.65 s wait. The 0x3ffff arm is real and correctly identified, but harmless: it is never waited out.
  - evidence: w4, 7932 arms of pc=1cb70 with count=262143. Time from each such arm to the MSC's NEXT repeat arm of any kind: median 33.7 ms, p90 40.0 ms, max 1621 ms, and only 3 of 7932 over 100 ms. So the monitor keeps cycling roughly every 34 ms and the 8.65 s count is not the source of the ~1053 ms completion latency. This is what the 'by design' reading predicts: if FIOMDLY's @RAW is met then every BCE is idle, so FIOMBCEM's buses are idle too and FIOMNTR6's @RAW is satisfied at once through its own +2 exit, whatever count it armed. The met-exit skipping '@LI 0' is therefore NOT a defect, and neither the mask (entry 2, refuted twice) nor this count explains the holds. WHAT THE NUMBERS NOW POINT AT, arithmetic fit only and NOT yet tested: FIOMNTR0 takes its monitor mask from the TOP ACTIVE IOQE (TCVTMIOA, 'GET I/O MONITOR MASK FROM THE TOP ACTIVE IOQE'), so the MSC watches one request at a time; at ~34 ms per monitor cycle a request 31 places down the queue would not be looked at for ~1053 ms, and 1053/34 = 31. That would make the hold a QUEUE DEPTH effect rather than a lost completion, and would move the question back to why so many IOQEs are outstanding -- the DEU fill storm -- rather than to the MSC or the BCE. Test it by recording queue depth (YAGPC_IOQEDEPTH exists) against hold length in the same run.
  - addrs `1cb6a-1cb71,14d-14d` &middot; csects `FIOMNTR,FIOMCNTL,FIOCMPLT` &middot; instrs `@RAW,@LI,@XAX,@L` &middot; procs `MSC` &middot; env `YAGPC_REPEATTRACE,YAGPC_IOQEDEPTH` &middot; files `src/iop_msc_instr.c,SSSRC/FIOMCNTL.asm` &middot; symptom `1053 ms DK holds,completion latency` &middot; tsec `380-400` &middot; doc `FIOMCNTL.asm FIOMDLY/FIOMCKIO comments` &middot; run `w4`
- **#40** *(iop)* WITHDRAWN in part. The claim that FIOMDLY and FIOMCKIO 'must be the same instruction' was over-stated: they sit at different addresses (1cb6c, 1cb70) and nothing requires identical encodings. The fall-through works because @RAW's time-out exit advances +1 into whatever follows. Both disassemble to d800 because both use displacement 0 with indexing -- a consequence, not a requirement. WHAT STANDS: exec_DLY is a no-op, and the POO documents @DLY as a real delay (18-bit EC, 2 us resolution, max ~0.54 s). LATENT: PASS uses @DLY nowhere.
  - evidence: src/iop_msc_instr.c exec_DLY is 'iop_incr_nia(t, 1);' and nothing else; the BCE's #DLY/#DLYI are separate and ARE implemented. Instruction census over SSSRC: @RAW 4 uses, @RNW 0, @RNI 0, @RAI 0, @DLY 0. So PASS uses exactly one repeat instruction for both the delay and the I/O-complete check, and the MSC bit is what distinguishes them. The POO also documents a pure-delay alternative the software does NOT take: 'A zero accumulator will never allow an @RNI or @RNW to reach their repeat conditions. Consequently, each repeat will loop until the maximum repeat count is reached.' Why @RAW was preferred over @RNW-with-zero or @DLY is not recorded in the source and should not be guessed at. NOTE on the two POO format lines: 'I=0 Count / @RAW COUNT' and 'I=1 X + Count / @RAW COUNT (1)' are the non-indexed and indexed forms of the COUNT, not a delay variant -- both still test the condition. FIOMDLY uses the indexed form because its count is computed at run time from TCVTMTTG and moved to the index register by @XAX, which a literal displacement could not carry.
  - addrs `1be4b-1be4b,14d-14d` &middot; csects `FIOMNTR,FIOSVC` &middot; instrs `@DLY,@RAW,@RNW,@RNI,@RAI` &middot; procs `MSC,BCE6` &middot; env `YAGPC_WATCHHW,YAGPC_PGMTRACE` &middot; tsec `390-397` &middot; run `w3,v91a`
- **#43** *(flight-sw)* The store-protect fault comes from an overlay's re-protection landing on memory something else still needs to write -- i.e. FIOMGCV's asynchronous verify-and-reprotect racing another writer.
  - evidence: Testable and tested against #PFCMGPT, negative. NONE of the addresses in the fault is inside any phase's load block: 080ce (the FPMSVCEP sentinel), 080d6 (the lastProt address) and 1be4b (the faulting instruction in FIOSVC) are all outside every overlay region, so their protection is part of the resident image and no overlay re-protected them. The IOQE pool itself (090b2..09273) is likewise covered by NO load block of any phase, so an overlay cannot corrupt or re-protect it. FPMIDLE, FIOCMPLT and FIOMGCV are also outside all overlay regions. The only I/O control structures any overlay covers are TCVTIOFP (0x14d) and TCVTBCEB (0x152), and only through phase 10's LB1 (00000..03c21) -- phase 10 is the IPL phase, loaded long before the transition. THE NON-RACE VERSION OF THE SAME IDEA IS NOT REFUTED AND IS THE LEADING HYPOTHESIS: see the entry on FIOMGCV's PCT.
  - addrs `080ce-08100,090b2-09273,1be4b-1be4b` &middot; csects `FIOMGCV,FIOSVC,FPMSVCEP` &middot; phases `8` &middot; run `w1`
- **#47** *(iop)* The DK completion latency is TCVTMIOA (the active-IOQE head) STALLING -- the head sitting on one entry far longer than that entry's I/O takes, so the defect would be in what advances it (FIOMGCMP/FIOCMPLT dequeuing).
  - evidence: REFUTED by direct measurement. Run z6, YAGPC_WATCHHW=146-152 covering TCVTMIOA (0x146) and TCVTBCEB (0x152) in one run so head changes correlate with bus-6 holds. Inside the 1053 ms hold at 391.833 the head changed 69 TIMES and the longest interval with it unchanged was 35.2 ms; the 2101 ms hold shows 135 changes, same 35 ms ceiling. The head is not stuck and advances briskly throughout. WHAT IS TRUE INSTEAD: the head cycles among a handful of IOQEs and bus 6's entry is almost never selected -- one check of bus 6 against 69 head changes. AND ONE ENTRY DOMINATES: address 9068 is at the head 33 of 69 times in that hold, 33 of 70 in the next, and 65 of 135 in the 2101 ms one -- about 48% consistently -- while the others (905a, 90c4, 90fa, 910c, 9130, 9166, 9178, 918a, 91d2, 922c) rotate. IOQE stride appears to be 18 halfwords. SO THE REMAINING QUESTION IS NARROWER: not why the head is slow, but why one IOQE keeps being re-selected as head while bus 6's waits. Note this is still distinct from the refuted queue-DEPTH idea, which turned on how many entries exist.
  - addrs `146-146,152-152,9068-9068,905a-922c` &middot; csects `FIOMNTR,FIOMGCMP,FIOCMPLT` &middot; procs `MSC,BCE6` &middot; env `YAGPC_WATCHHW` &middot; tsec `390-396` &middot; run `z6`
- **#51** *(emulator)* Porting upstream's coordinated set (zero receive-timeout floor + 33 us MIA receive pacing + @RAW keeping accumulator bit 0) fixes the DK holds. The reasoning was that bit 0 alone had been catastrophic because it was one item of an interdependent set.
  - evidence: REFUTED by run u1, YAGPC_IOP_UPSTREAM=1, full length. The set regresses EXACTLY as the mask alone did: IPL is healthy (all reads through 228 blocks at 128.9 s and 38 at 136.5 s), then the IOQE sentinel fault PGMCHK 0007 at=1be4b lastProt=080d6 fires at t=231.19 and 232.03 -- against x2's 230.9 for the mask alone -- no transition, no overlay landmarks, and the bus-6 hold distribution goes to 15 intervals with a 2166 ms MEDIAN and 13 over 300 ms, against a baseline of 804 intervals at 0.85 ms with 5 over 300 ms. So the pacing and the zero floor do NOT rescue bit 0, and entry 2's refutation of the mask stands on its own rather than being an artifact of isolation. WHAT IS STILL WORTH TESTING, and is now split out behind YAGPC_MIA_PACE (the zero floor needs no flag, YAGPC_RECV_FLOOR_US=0 does it): the pacing and the floor WITHOUT the mask change (run u2). Those two are independently justified -- FCMINIOP programs per-bus MTOs that our 2 ms floor overrides, and a real MIA presents one word per bus word time -- and they may help even though bit 0 does not. IF u2 IS ALSO BAD, the remaining items of 818df88 are load-bearing here and the port must be wider: the sector replacing the high-order bit of a 16-bit address, SRS/SI keeping the low bit of the base, SVC saving the sector in old-PSW bits 40-43, BALR/SCAL taking the link before the branch address, ISPB M1=001/011 pairing (EA, EA+1), @LH sign-extending, and the IOP stepping on elapsed time.
  - instrs `@RAW` &middot; procs `MSC,BCE6` &middot; env `YAGPC_IOP_UPSTREAM,YAGPC_MIA_PACE,YAGPC_RECV_FLOOR_US` &middot; files `src/iop.c,src/iop.h,src/iop_msc_instr.c` &middot; tsec `230-232` &middot; doc `nsts-sim-gpc 818df88` &middot; run `u1,u2,x2`
- **#52** *(emulator)* The ~1053 ms DK holds are caused by our receive timing -- the 2 ms RECV_TIMEOUT_FLOOR_US overriding the per-bus MTOs FCMINIOP programs, and/or the absence of 33 us MIA receive pacing.
  - evidence: REFUTED by run u2: YAGPC_MIA_PACE=1 with YAGPC_RECV_FLOOR_US=0, i.e. both corrections WITHOUT the @RAW mask change. The run is valid (transition at 390.55/392.23/397.87, phase 3's overlay completes at 391.489) and the hold distribution is unchanged from baseline: 804 intervals with 5 over 300 ms and a 4263.80 ms maximum, against baseline's 804 / 5 / 4264. Only the median moves, 0.85 -> 1.36 ms, which is the pacing showing up on short transfers as it should. Phase 8's overlay still never completes, cksum-verify still fires twice (both for phase 3's transaction), and the sentinel fault still lands at 396.91. SO THE TWO CORRECTIONS ARE SAFE AND FAITHFUL BUT ARE NOT THE FIX. They are independently justified -- FCMINIOP programs per-bus MTOs (33 us most buses, 49.5 us bus 24, 5 ms DK, 1.959936 s mass memory) that a 2 ms floor overrides 40-60x, and nsts-sim-gpc 818df88 makes both corrections -- so they are worth keeping on their own merits, but they do not touch the holds. CONTRAST with the @RAW mask, which is ALSO documented as required and which does NOT leave the machine standing (entry 2).
  - procs `BCE6,BCE24,MSC` &middot; env `YAGPC_MIA_PACE,YAGPC_RECV_FLOOR_US` &middot; files `src/iop.c,SSSRC/FCMINIOP.asm` &middot; doc `nsts-sim-gpc 818df88` &middot; run `u1,u2`
- **#66** *(flight-sw)* The zero entries in the SVC entry-point table FPMSVCEP (SVCs 0, 10, 11, 23, 28, 29; the 'unresolved FSVC00nn' relocations of every link) are a link defect.
  - evidence: All eight DASS dumps have 0000 in exactly those six entries too; the other entries match ours exactly. They are unused SVC numbers. A machine that issues one of them branches to 0 through FPMSVC (LA R2,X'80CE' / LH R2,0(R4,R2) / BAL R7,0(R2)), which is what OPS 901 on v38 does at 274.6 s -- so the question is why an unused SVC is issued, not the table.
  - addrs `80ce-80f5,1b13a-1b15a` &middot; csects `FPMSVCEP,FPMSVC` &middot; tape `v38` &middot; run `R901`
- **#69** *(emulator)* exec_BCR is wrong because 'BR 7' (BCR 7,R7) does not branch when CC=2. THE BCR IS CORRECT -- do not 'fix' it.
  - evidence: AP-101S instruction set sec. 5.3: 'bit 5 tests for a code equal 00, bit 6 tests for a code equal 11, and bit 7 tests for a code equal 01... M1 = 111 always branches' -- and the programming-notes table shows every instruction class producing only 00, 11 or 01. CC=10 arises only from the documented CC ANOMALY (Figure 2-20 note '##'): a MASKED DMA STORE PROTECT interrupt 'will set the condition code (CC) to a binary 10... This can result in erroneous GPC operation of an instruction which tries to utilize the CC... before they are set by another instruction'. Our two psw_set_cc(...,2) sites are exactly that note (cc_anomaly and cpu_signal_dma_protect_violation). MEASURED in L901 (v39, OPS 901, landmark stop at FPMIHIM t=285.438): R7 = a0ac0031, the CORRECT return into FIOLGERR, and FP$GMTIM's saved R7 is the same; the Instruction Monitor old PSW at 0x0070 is all zero, so NO interrupt was taken; PSW1 = acf88031, CC=2. So FPMGMTIM's closing c7e7 (BCR 7,R7) fell through to the next halfword, which is FPMIHIM+0 -- as the real machine would. The defect is upstream: something DMAs into protected storage.
  - addrs `1acf7,1acf8,932e,70-77` &middot; csects `FPMGMTIM,FPMIHIM,FIOLGERR` &middot; instrs `BCR,BC` &middot; tape `v39` &middot; doc `AP-101S-instruction-set.txt sec 5.3; Figure 2-20 note ##` &middot; run `L901`
- **#91** *(emulator)* The inter-GPC sync codes reaching a neighbour by UDP could not meet FCOS's 3.85 ms timeout. SETTLED BY REMOVING THE PATH, not by measuring it: the rotated bits now go straight into the neighbour's register from the sending machine's thread (discretes_apply_external), synchronously within the sender's call, so there is no delivery latency left to miss a deadline. The datagram is still sent, for external monitors only.
  - evidence: A WARNING ABOUT THE MEASUREMENT THAT WAS ATTEMPTED FIRST, so nobody repeats it: pairing a receive-side trace against a transmit-side trace by (sender, code) IS NOT A LATENCY MEASUREMENT. The sync code alternates between just two values, null and SSIP, thousands of times a run, so an arriving trace pairs against whichever recent transmission happens to carry the same code. That instrument produced '28.7% late' before the fix and '81.6% late' after it -- both artefacts, and the second is what an instrument looks like when it is measuring itself. A sound version needs a per-transition sequence number carried from sender to receiver, not a code match. What is true by construction: vehicle_route_out now calls discretes_apply_external in the sender's thread before returning, so the neighbour's register holds the new bits before the sending instruction completes, and the generation bump invalidates the receiver's overlay cache immediately. Any remaining delay is the receiving CPU's own interval between reads of discrete input A, which is a different quantity from the one this entry was about.
  - csects `FCMCSYNC,FCMTSYNC,FCMSSYNC,FCMISYNC,FCMSFAIL,FCMSMASK,FCMASYNC` &middot; env `YAGPC_BARRIER_US` &middot; files `src/discretes.c,src/vehicle.c` &middot; symptom `fail to sync,GPC voted out,3.85 ms timeout`
- **#103** *(emulator)* WRONG DIRECTION -- DO NOT REUSE. This entry said the OPS request cannot reach a second computer because the intercomputer bus is write-only, making ICC receive the blocker. The flight software says the dependency runs the OTHER WAY: the ICC is DOWNSTREAM of the common set, not upstream of it. SSSRC/FIOICCPG.asm has one GPC run FIOSICCM, the 'SSIP ICC COMMANDER PROGRAM (WRITE IT OUT)', while the others run FIOSICLS, the 'SSIP ICC LISTENER PROGRAM' -- and the listener issues NO COMMAND WORD AT ALL, just #RDLI to read the data. So the ICC is a broadcast the listeners simply receive, those programs are dispatched as part of SSIP sync processing, and SSIP sync processing is something only a COMMON SET MEMBER does. A computer that is in no set never runs the listener, which is why ours never reads.
  - evidence: FIOICCPG.asm:76-93. FIOSICCM: #LBR@ FIOBRE 'THIS GPC BUFFER', #DLYI 0 'WAIT FOR LISTENER', #MOUT 0,SIPICCNT-1 'WRITE OUT OUR BUFFER'. FIOSICLS: #LBR@ FIOBRE 'BUFFER FOR INPUT GPC', #RDLI SIPICCNT-1 'READ THE DATA'. SIPICCNT is 124 words, so neither is the 31-word transfer this entry decoded -- those four reads belong to something else and are recorded in #107. THE CONSEQUENCE FOR THE EMULATOR: building an ICC that answers read commands was solving a problem the software does not have. What the wire has to do is deliver a broadcast to listeners, which is what iccmodel.c already does; what is missing is upstream, in the common set. SO THE BLOCKER IS BACK WHERE #106 PUT IT: the set never forms on the DISCRETES, TCVTCSSM stays zero, and FCMSMASK is called once per machine at load with an empty mask.
  - csects `DM6OPS,CZ2COMMO,ARCGPC` &middot; procs `BCE24` &middot; files `src/iccmodel.c,src/run.c` &middot; symptom `ICC,intercomputer bus,recvWords 0,redundant set,DM6OPS targets` &middot; run `rs11`
- **#114** *(goal)* THE TWO COMPUTERS DO NOT DO THE SAME I/O, BY A FACTOR OF SEVENTY, which is why the I/O-complete sync can never be met. In one 900 s run GPC1 enters FCMISYNC 34,432 times and GPC2 enters it 487 times. A barrier that every member must reach cannot be reached by a computer that arrives at it seventy times less often, so the set is voted apart on the first one after admission.
  - evidence: Run io1, tracing FCMISYNC's entry address 0x18e94 on both machines: 34432 entries for gpc=1 against 487 for gpc=2. The cause is not subtle -- GPC1 commands the display (1848 DEU commands in the equivalent single-GPC run), the mass memory and the timing unit, while GPC2 sits in GPC MEMORY commanding essentially nothing. AND THIS IS CIRCULAR IN THE EMULATOR AS IT STANDS: a computer needs bus assignments to do comparable I/O, the assignments come from the NBAT, and the NBAT takes effect at an OPS transition that the computer can only join if it is in the set. The real vehicle breaks the circle because the NBAT's assignments apply to every TARGET GPC as the transition runs, not only to the one that typed it. NOTE WHAT IS ALREADY EXCLUDED so this is not re-litigated: the barrier keeps the clocks within 200 us with zero abandoned holds (#113), the sync code and its timing windows are correct (#109), and handing GPC2 a display unit of its own does not change the ratio or the outcome (#113). The disagreement is in HOW MUCH I/O each computer does, not in when either of them does it. addrs 18e94-18e94 | csects FCMISYNC,ARCGPC,DM6OPS | procs BCE6,BCE18,BCE20 | symptom I/O complete sync,70 to 1,different I/O,set voted apart,redundant set | run io1 ANSWERED FROM THE SOURCE, so it need not be guessed: FCMISYNC.asm:33-36 says it 'SYNCHRONIZES GPCS IN THE COMMON OR REDUNDANT SETS FOR I/O COMPLETION (IOC) INTERRUPTS'. So a common-set member IS meant to take part, and GPC2's inclusion is correct rather than something the emulator invented. That makes the 70-to-1 disparity the thing to explain, and it points back at how much I/O OUR GPC1 generates rather than at how little GPC2 does -- 34,432 entries in 900 s is 38 I/O completions a second for a computer sitting on the GPC MEMORY display. Ledger #22 measured a DK hold as 1052 ms of pure dispatch latency with the BCE finishing in 0.3 ms, and #49 measured bus 24 taking more transactions than any modelled bus; either would inflate completions on the busy computer. Counting FCMISYNC entries PER BUS is the measurement that separates them, and it has not been made. addrs 18e94-18e94 | csects FCMISYNC,ARCGPC,DM6OPS | procs BCE6,BCE18,BCE20 | symptom I/O complete sync,70 to 1,different I/O,set voted apart,redundant set | run io1  SUPERSEDED BY #117, WHICH MEASURED THE BARRIERS RATHER THAN THE ENTRIES. The 34,432-against-487 figure is real and reproducible (34,428 against 454 in run ms2), but it counts CALLS TO FCMISYNC, and a call is not a barrier. FCMISYNC picks its mask from TIOQSYNT in the requesting IOQE; the redundant-set mask is zero on both machines, so every one of those passes takes the `R3 = 0` exit at 0x18eb0 and waits for nobody. The passes that actually enter the spin number FOUR ON EACH MACHINE. The two computers therefore reach the same number of I/O-complete barriers, and "a barrier that every member must reach cannot be reached by a computer that arrives at it seventy times less often" does not describe this failure. DO NOT RE-ADOPT the entry-count disparity as the cause.
  - addrs `18e94-18e94` &middot; csects `FCMISYNC,ARCGPC,DM6OPS` &middot; procs `BCE6,BCE18,BCE20` &middot; symptom `I/O complete sync,70 to 1,different I/O,set voted apart,redundant set` &middot; run `io1`
- **#116** *(emulator)* ATTACHING MORE DISPLAY UNITS DOES NOT FIX THE I/O INFLATION -- IT MAKES IT WORSE. The idea was that PASS drives four display units and modelling one leaves the DK handler talking to units that never answer, so giving it DK2, DK3 and DK4 would bring the busy computer's I/O down. Tested: GPC1's total transactions ROSE, and buses that had been silent opened up with millions of their own.
  - evidence: Run bc2, identical to bc1 but with --deu-bus 7,8,9. Bus 7 did fall as predicted, 561,010 to 161,916, so attaching a unit really does slow its bus. But bus 8 barely moved (561,332 to 522,167) and the software, now getting further -- mmu1 reads 1040 blocks against 749 -- opened buses that had had NOTHING before: bus 14 = 3,214,762 and bus 15 = 3,211,341 where both were absent from the bc1 census entirely, bus 18 = 3,212,932 (was 2,153,932), bus 20 = 4,750,010 (was 541,708), bus 21 = 2,753,097 (was 17,976), bus 24 = 2,534,724 (was 2,036,905). GPC2 is unchanged at bus 19 = 1,022,695 and bus 6 = 11,316, so the gap WIDENED. THE LESSON, and it is the useful part: the inflation is not about which peripherals exist but about what an unserved bus COSTS, and every new bus the software reaches inherits the same defect. So the fix has to be in the bus timing itself -- #115's proposal -- and not in the vehicle's inventory. Do not retry this by adding models.
  - procs `BCE7,BCE8,BCE14,BCE15,BCE20,BCE21,BCE24` &middot; env `--deu-bus,YAGPC_BUSCENSUS` &middot; files `src/run.c,src/iop.c` &middot; symptom `unmodelled bus,inflated I/O,more DEUs,fail to sync` &middot; run `bc1,bc2`
- **#125** *(emulator)* THE SIMULATED-TIME BARRIER IS NOT WHY THE SET BREAKS, and tightening it does not help. The handshake pulse is 143 us wide and the barrier's default tolerance is 200 us, so a reader may in principle step over a pulse entirely -- a clean quantitative story, and wrong. At 25 us, where the permitted drift is a sixth of the pulse, the set is voted apart exactly as before. DO NOT RETUNE THE BARRIER AGAINST THE PULSE WIDTH; the skew that matters is not simulated-time drift.
  - evidence: Sweep of YAGPC_BARRIER_US over three 400 s two-computer runs, otherwise identical (runs sf-short, bar25, bar5). 200 us (default): 3 fail-votes, mmu2 432 blocks, 8 ICC commands per bus, barrier held 457,494 times for 128.1 s, 0 abandoned. 25 us: 3 FAIL-VOTES STILL, mmu2 432 blocks, only 2 ICC commands per bus, barrier held 2,742,259 times for 322.9 s of a 400 s run. 5 us: 0 fail-votes BUT THAT IS NOT A PASS -- mmu2 read 160 blocks of 432, so GPC2 never finished loading PASS and never reached a sync at all; the barrier held 2,082,125 times for 224.2 s. So the only run without a vote is the one that never got far enough to have one, and the only effect of tightening the barrier is to spend the run waiting. WHAT THE FAILING WAIT ACTUALLY SEES, traced instruction by instruction on GPC2 (run sp4, YAGPC_RANGETRACE over 0x18ece-0x18f10): every one of the ~96 samples in the 3.85 ms spin reads R3=0x27000111, GPC1 sitting at 111 null, sampled every 39-40 us. FCMADIS contributes 0x0000f000. The masked term R2=0x111 and the comparison at 0x18f07 gives R4=0 on EVERY iteration -- the condition is satisfied throughout -- and the loop is left not by the condition but by the timer at 0x18f0e. The clock read by ICR counts DOWN at one count per microsecond; R1 = start - FCMSNTO2, the difference goes negative after 3807 us, and the second phase at 0x18f32 then re-reads the discretes, finds no NEW neighbour bit, and falls to 0x18f44 BAL FCMSFINT and 0x18f4d BAL FCMSFAIL. So GPC1 does not raise the I/O-complete code at any point during GPC2's whole 3.85 ms wait; the SYNC trace shows its 001 lasting 143 us and ending BEFORE that wait began. GPC2 arrives late by more than a pulse width, and at a barrier delta of 25 us that lateness cannot be simulated-time drift. WHAT IS LEFT: the emulator's own completion-dispatch latency, #22 -- a hold that is entirely the gap between the BCE finishing its bus program in 0.3 ms and FCOS learning about it. #115 predicted this order of work and this measurement confirms it.  CORRECTED 2026-09-13 by #126: 'GPC2 arrives late by more than a pulse width' generalised from ONE handshake and is withdrawn. A swarm of eight runs shows no systematic lateness, and at the failing handshake both computers raise I/O-complete within 13-143 us of each other; the failure is the voter not recognising a present partner. The sweep result itself stands -- 25 us still failed -- but the pointer to #22 that followed from 'late' is unsupported.
  - addrs `18ece-18f10,18f44-18f4f` &middot; csects `FCMISYNC,FCMSFINT,FCMSFAIL` &middot; env `YAGPC_BARRIER_US,YAGPC_RANGETRACE,YAGPC_SYNCTRACE` &middot; files `src/vehicle.c,src/vehicle.h` &middot; symptom `fail to sync,barrier delta,pulse width,I/O complete sync,arrives late,completion latency` &middot; run `sf-short,sp4,bar25,bar5`
- **#169** *(method)* PORTING MEDS2.py TO C IS NOT JUSTIFIED BY PERFORMANCE -- but NOT for the reason first given. The emulator DOES stop and wait in wall-clock time for a display reply, so MEDS's LATENCY gates it directly whatever MEDS's CPU use is; arguing from 'MEDS runs at 5% CPU, therefore irrelevant' confuses throughput with latency and is wrong. Measured, the waiting costs 0.8% of a run.
  - evidence: iop.c:1965 is explicit: 'A PEER IN ANOTHER PROCESS IS LATE IN WALL TIME, NOT IN SIMULATED TIME' -- once a bus reply is more than PEER_HOLD_AFTER_US (500 us) overdue, bcenet_framer_peer_wait holds the machine until it arrives, and rtpacer_note_peer_hold counts it. MEASURED with YAGPC_TIMEOUT_TRACE (which costs nothing per instruction, unlike YAGPC_PACETRACE, whose four clock reads per step would slow the emulator and so give MEDS more time, understating the very thing being measured), one GPC three displays, 428 s: 808 holds totalling 3.55 s = 0.8% OF THE RUN; per hold min 0.01 ms, MEDIAN 4.92 ms, max 87.29 ms. So MEDS is VERY late when it is late -- a real display unit answers in tens of microseconds -- but rarely enough that the total is under one per cent. WHY IT IS LATE: MEDS2.py drains its socket from the Qt event loop (QSocketNotifier -> _readable), so while it is painting it is not reading, and a reply waits behind a paint. THE CHEAP FIX IF IT EVER MATTERS is a dedicated receive thread decoupling drain from paint -- about 50 lines, in Python, since the GIL is released during the blocking recv and during Qt's painting -- NOT an 11898-line rewrite. SCALING: at rate 0.2 this is 0.8%; at real time the bus traffic is ~5x denser so holds might cost ~4%, still not a case for a port. PORTABILITY IS NO LONGER THE OBJECTION IT WAS: the GUI is Qt, so a C++ port keeps the same toolkit and platform behaviour, and .github/workflows/ci.yml already builds on macos-latest, so the platforms the user does not own are covered by CI.
  - env `YAGPC_TIMEOUT_TRACE` &middot; files `discretePanel/MEDS2.py,src/iop.c,src/rtpacer.c` &middot; symptom `peer hold,display latency,MEDS slow,port to C`
- **#171** *(method)* THE BARRIER DELTA CANNOT SAFELY BE RAISED ABOVE 50 us, and PROCESS-WIDE CPU PINNING MAKES THINGS WORSE. Raising YAGPC_BARRIER_US looked like a 20% saving until the CAM was checked: at 100, 150 and 200 us a fail-vote is left STANDING. 25 and 50 us clear their votes, and 50 buys only about 10 points, which is inside run-to-run spread. CHECK THE CAM, NOT THE EMULATOR LOG: yaGPC2.log never prints 'fail vote' or 'fail to sync', so grepping it for those reports zero every time and is not a check at all.
  - evidence: Two GPCs to OPS 201 on a 2011 i7-3930K, CPU sampled for 40-60 s once settled, CAM read from cam.log. delta 25: 213%, both votes cleared. delta 50: 203-210%, all cleared, barrier 8.59M holds / 109.3 s / 8.55M spin-released / 50634 sleeps. delta 100: 169%, STANDING VOTE GPC2->GPC1. delta 150: 189%, STANDING VOTE GPC1->GPC2. delta 200: 170%, STANDING VOTE GPC2->GPC1. The CPU figures are NOT monotonic in delta (100 gives 169% but 150 gives 189%) and identical configurations differ by 7 points between runs, so single runs cannot resolve the effect anyway. THE SPIN IS NOT WASTE: at delta 50 the average hold is 12.7 us and 99.6% release during the spin, while an OS sleep cannot resolve anything under ~50 us -- so sleeping instead would cost more. About 11% of thread time goes to barrier holds at delta 50 and that looks close to irreducible without loosening delta. PINNING, AS TESTED, IS HARMFUL AND THE TEST WAS WRONG: 'taskset -c 0,2,4,6,8,10' restricts the WHOLE simulation to six CPUs -- three MEDS, panel, CAM and the transport thread as well as the two GPC threads -- so the display clients then compete with the computers instead of using the other six logical CPUs. Result at delta 50: holds 8.59M -> 31.9M, hold time 109 s -> 224 s, and a STANDING VOTE where the unpinned run had none. Steps went 378M/336M (balanced) to 256M/966M, a 3.8x imbalance -- a dropped-out computer spinning, not work done, which is also why TOTAL STEPS ROSE WHILE PROGRESS FELL. A correct test would pin ONLY the two GPC threads to distinct physical cores, which needs per-thread affinity, not a process-wide mask.
  - env `YAGPC_BARRIER_US` &middot; files `discretePanel/simulatePASS.py,src/vehicle.c` &middot; symptom `standing vote,CAM,barrier spin,taskset,hyperthread`
- **#181** *(flight-sw)* 'GPC MEMORY 1' ON CRT2 DURING AND AFTER AN IPL IS NOT A BUG -- it names the GPC that really is driving CRT2, and the assignment itself is what the vehicle's own rule prescribes. DPS Workbook USA005350 Rev B, DK Assignment: 'the GPC currently in control of that IDP transfers control to the GPC specified for that IDP (CRT) in the active NBAT for that major function. IF NO GPC IS SPECIFIED FOR THAT IDP, THE LOWEST NUMBERED GPC IN THAT MAJOR FUNCTION ASSUMES CONTROL.' During an IPL no NBAT entry exists for CRT2, so CRT2 falls to GPC1. The digit is the commanding GPC (DPS Dictionary, GPCIPL MENU note 3: 'The GPC driving the display is indicated'). Consistent with the owner's own observation that OPS 000 PRO from OPS 201 shows GPC MEMORY 2: by then the NBAT names GPC2 for CRT2. The GPCIPL MENU digit tracks 2,3,4 because the BFC CRT select routes the CRT to the GPC being IPLed.
  - evidence: Measured with YAGPC_XMITENA_TRACE over a 4-GPC 2-CRT IPL: GPC1 holds the MIA transmitters for BCE6 (CRT1) and BCE7 (CRT2) from t=57 s and drops BCE7 only at the OPS 201 transition (t=423 s); GPC2/3/4 never enable a display transmitter during the IPL. In a 2-GPC run stopped in OPS 0 (examples/2gpc-ops0-gpccrt.script) CRT2 reads 'GPC MEMORY' with GPC1 commanding, and after GPC/CRT 2 2 EXEC it reads '0001/ / GPC MEMORY 2'.
  - csects `FCMBMASK,FCMBUSCM` &middot; procs `BCE6,BCE7` &middot; env `YAGPC_XMITENA_TRACE` &middot; symptom `GPC MEMORY 1 on CRT2,wrong GPC number on display,CRT2 shows GPC1` &middot; doc `DPS Workbook USA005350 Rev B (DK Assignment); DPS Dictionary Rev J (GPCIPL MENU)` &middot; run `dknum,gpccrt`
- **#183** *(emulator)* WRONG AS FIRST WRITTEN, AND THE ERROR WAS IN THE INSTRUMENT, NOT THE VEHICLE. I claimed a bus reassignment reaches only the computer that owns the keyboard, because no GPC but GPC1 was ever seen enabling a display transmitter. The trace pads its verb to 'ENABLE ' with two spaces and my parser required one, so it matched only DISABLE lines -- 539 of 678 lines dropped in silence, and every 'never enables' statement came from that. WHAT THE TRACE ACTUALLY SHOWS (same logs, correct parser): GPC2 DOES take CRT2, enabling its transmitter inside FCMBUSCM's enable path (nia=185e4) at t=50.0 s in the GPC/CRT run, t=82.8 s in the NBAT-first run and t=340.9 s in the 4-GPC run -- and GPC1 does NOT release it until much later (t=103.3 s at the GPC/CRT entry, t=234.9 s and t=423.1 s at the OPS transition). So for a long stretch BOTH computers command CRT2. Superseded by #184, which states that as the defect to chase.
  - evidence: dknum/gpccrt/nbatfirst3 with YAGPC_XMITENA_TRACE; 678, 225 and 245 events parse with the corrected pattern against 139, 43 and (unknown) before. The end-of-run MIA masks agree with the corrected reading: GPC2 xmit=21013480 includes bus 7, which the broken parser's last-seen mask (20013480) did not.
  - addrs `185ba,18308,183f6,185f0` &middot; csects `FCMBUSCM,FCMBMAN,FCMASYNC,FCMCSYNC` &middot; procs `BCE6,BCE7` &middot; env `YAGPC_XMITENA_TRACE,YAGPC_LANDMARKS_MAX` &middot; files `src/iop.c,src/iop_bce_instr.c` &middot; symptom `POLL FAIL after GPC/CRT,no commander for a CRT,new commander never enables its transmitter` &middot; run `buscm2,gpccrt,dknum`
- **#186** *(emulator)* REFUTED, AND IT WAS MY OWN CLOCK ERROR BOTH TIMES.  The claim was that several GPCs' bootstraps talk on the display buses at once, before any of them is IPLed -- GPC2, GPC3 and GPC4 all commanding BCE7 from t=13.7 s while GPC1 is being loaded.  Measured on the VEHICLE'S SHARED CLOCK, no such thing happens: bus 7 carries no traffic at all before t=59.6, and each machine's bootstrap talks only AFTER ITS OWN IPL.  The number 13.697 is not an event time at all -- it is the fixed offset from a machine leaving reset to its bootstrap's first display-bus command, so EVERY machine prints t=13.697 on its own clock no matter when in the run it was loaded.  Reading those four identical numbers as one instant is what produced both wrong findings here: first 'machines in HALT transmit', then 'three bootstraps at once'.  There is no defect.  A bootstrap talking on DK1 after its own IPL is run.c's documented behaviour.
  - evidence: Run 4gpc2 (4 GPCs, 2 CRTs, corrected subtitled script, YAGPC_CMDTRACE=6,7, 9400 command words, 0 parser rejects).  First command on bus 7 per machine, own clock vs shared: gpc1 13.697/59.550, gpc2 13.697/97.965, gpc3 13.697/183.788, gpc4 13.697/269.679 -- identical on the own clock, 210 s apart on the shared one, each following that machine's own MODE: RUN.  Bus 6: gpc1 only, 13.697/13.697 (it is the machine whose reset release defines the shared origin, which is why the two agree for it alone and made the artifact look like data).  Shared-clock seconds with two commanders on bus 6 or bus 7: 0 of 627 and 0 of 447.
  - procs `BCE6,BCE7,IOP` &middot; env `YAGPC_CMDTRACE,YAGPC_HELDTRACE` &middot; files `src/run.c,src/iop.c` &middot; symptom `dual commanders,HALT machine transmits,commands from a computer in reset` &middot; tsec `13-670` &middot; doc `AP-101S PoO, DI-0 HALT holds the CPU in system reset` &middot; run `4gpc2,heldchk,4gpcfull2`

## Superseded

- **#61** *(method)* BUILD PROVENANCE IS NOT KEPT: the tape that a run used cannot be traced back to the tree that made it. Staging is reused in place, so c80boot/ and c80src/ hold only what the LAST build left; 36 numbered volumes were cut across 09-08/09-09 and only the .mmv of each survives. Do not assume a phase*/ or c80boot/ artifact corresponds to any particular tape -- CHECK, by fingerprinting a few halfwords.
  - evidence: WRONG AS STATED: v36's own staging tree survives at /tmp/claude-1000/c80v36 (09-09 14:53), with a .lnk101.repro.json per phase recording every input and its md5; I searched ~/pass-build and c80boot but never c80v*. The real provenance gaps were different: the build script lived only in /tmp/claude-1000/buildtape.sh, several inputs had no recipe, one object (AIGDEU) had drifted, and dfg was an uncommitted working tree. All resolved by yaGPC2/tapebuild/ -- see the entry recording the from-source build.
  - tape `v36,v27,v35` &middot; symptom `cannot reproduce tape from tree`
- **#104** *(goal)* SUPERSEDED by #106, #111 and #112. This said two computers load and sync but never form a set, and that the cause was not delivery latency. The second half stands; the first stopped being true once the stale-level defect (#110) was fixed -- the set DOES form now, and is voted apart 4.33 ms later. Read #111 for the state and #112 for which sync fails.
  - evidence: Run rs12, identical script to rs9 but with discretes_apply_external in place: keys 15 of 15 delivered, mmu1 71/749, mmu2 59/432 -- the same figures as rs9, rs10 and rs11 before the change. So the socket was never the reason. TWO THINGS THE RUN DOES SAY. First, ONLY null and SSIP ever appear on the lines -- 6732 nulls and 6730 SSIPs across both machines, and NOT ONE timer (101), SVC (100), IPR (010) or I/O-complete (001) code. In a redundant set every timer tick and synchronising SVC is a barrier, so their total absence means neither machine believes it is in a set with anybody; they are each running their own SSIP cycle. Second, the intercomputer bus is still write-only: recvPolls 10 and recvCalls 0 against 57730 command words and 1477888 data words in 900 s, which is #103 unchanged. WHAT TO DO NEXT, and it is a measurement not a guess: CZ2B_CS and CZ2B_RS are the last two fields of the CZ2V_GST structure in CZ2COMMO.hal, one entry per GPC, and CZ2B_RS is literally the redundant-set mask -- reading either would settle in one run whether the common set forms. Getting at it needs the address, which needs a link map we do not currently produce for the whole image; the GPC MEMORY display's own READ/WRITE section (ITEM 28+addr shows a location in the ACTUAL column) is the alternative and needs no new tooling.
  - csects `CZ2COMMO,FCMASYNC,FCMCSYNC,DM6OPS` &middot; tape `v44` &middot; symptom `no common set,no redundant set,only SSIP and null,GPC2 not in OPS 2` &middot; run `rs12`
- **#149** *(emulator)* SUPERSEDED BY #148, which is the same thing seen from outside. A computer stops issuing sync codes and the others vote it out; it keeps RUN(READY) and I/O ACTIVE, still hears its neighbours, still burns a core, and holds the NULL code 111 (not 000 dead/halt/standby) for hours. With YAGPC_SYNCTRACE the cause is visible: it is #148's IPR-handshake slip. Seen twice, on GPC2 (2026-09-16 06:17) and GPC1 (13:02).
  - evidence: THE SECOND EPISODE, WITH THE TRACE ON, is the useful one. 4-GPC OPS 2 demo run, tape OI340700-v44boot, YAGPC_SYNCTRACE=1. GPC1's last transmission: t=794931.477193 'GPC1 out 111 null', 4.4 ms after a normal SVC sync (out 100 SVC at .472804, seen by all three peers). At .477458-.477483 GPC2, GPC3 and GPC4 each issued 010 IPR and GPC1 issued NONE; the computer fail lamp went on in the same millisecond; the other three returned to null and carried on. GPC1 has not transmitted since (checked 16:08, 3 h later: 0 OUT changes in 6 s against 3452/3451/3453 for the others). IPR traffic is normal and constant: 5096 IPRs in the 400 s before the failure, 1274 from each computer, about three a second each -- so this is one slip in thousands, matching #148's rate of one episode per run. The CAM showed 11 with votes 21, 31, 41 -- correct live behaviour after #150 removed the stale latch, which is how the owner noticed. FIRST EPISODE (no trace): GPC2 at 06:17:38 after the crew took the set to OPS 301, same aftermath, votes cleared at 08:11 when the survivors re-converged while GPC2 stayed silent at null.
  - csects `FCMCSYNC,FCMTSYNC,FCMSSYNC,FCMISYNC,FCMSFAIL` &middot; procs `CPU` &middot; config `G2` &middot; tape `v44boot` &middot; env `YAGPC_SYNCTRACE` &middot; symptom `CAM diagonal,voted out,sync code null,fail to sync,dual commanders,display blanks and redraws` &middot; run `demo-0916-0420`
